62633
67976



dizziness, headache, tinnitus


Headache and dizziness are some of the more common neurologic side effects of oral aprepitant or parenteral fosaprepitant in pooled analyses of adults receiving moderately (MEC) and highly emetogenic chemotherapy (HEC). Headache was reported in 2.2% to 3% of those receiving aprepitant or fosaprepitant in clinical trials for emetogenic chemotherapy, but rates were not significantly different from subjects receiving standard therapy. In pediatric patients who received MEC and HEC, 9% of subjects treated with aprepitant and ondansetron experienced headache, compared with 5% of those who received ondansetron alone. Headache was reported in 4% of subjects receiving IV aprepitant for the prevention of post-operative nausea and vomiting (PONV). Dizziness was reported in more than 0.5% of adults in pooled analyses of moderately (MEC) and highly emetogenic chemotherapy (HEC) clinical trials and at a greater incidence than comparative drugs. In pediatric patients who received aprepitant and ondansetron for MEC and HEC, 5% experienced dizziness, compared with 1% of those who received ondansetron alone. Tinnitus was reported in less than 1% of subjects receiving the 3-day oral aprepitant regimen for MEC, but with a greater incidence than subjects receiving standard therapy. In a pooled analysis of PONV studies, less common adverse reactions reported in more than 0.5% of subjects treated with oral aprepitant and at a greater incidence than ondansetron included dizziness.


injection site reaction, phlebitis, skin irritation, skin necrosis, vasculitis


In clinical trials of individuals receiving moderately or highly emetogenic chemotherapy (MEC or HEC), an injection site reaction occurred in 2.2% to 3% of individuals receiving a regimen including fosaprepitant IV infusion, compared to 0.5 to 0.6% of those treated with standard therapy. Infusion site reaction types reported in fosaprepitant-treated individuals included infusion site erythema (0.5% or less), site induration (0.2% or less), infusion pain (1.2% or less), localized pruritus (0.3% or less), thrombo-phlebitis (0.6% or less), skin irritation (0.2% or less), and vessel puncture site pain (0.2% or less). In addition, fosaprepitant-treated individuals have experienced localized edema, skin necrosis, and vasculitis at or near the infusion site. Usually, infusion site reactions occur within the first 3 exposures; in some cases reactions persisted for 2 weeks or longer. These reactions were separate from the systemic infusion reactions that may occur during or after fosaprepitant or aprepitant IV infusion. Avoid infusion of fosaprepitant into small veins or through a butterfly catheter. Closely monitor individuals during and after the infusion. Do not reinitiate fosaprepitant in individuals who experience infusion site reactions with first-time use.


constipation, ileus


In various clinical trials for prevention of post-operative nausea and vomiting (PONV) and PONV using either IV or PO aprepitant, constipation was reported in 8% and 9% of subjects receiving intravenous aprepitant and oral aprepitant, respectively. In addition, 2 serious adverse reactions were reported in PONV clinical studies of oral aprepitant in subjects taking a higher than recommended dose: 1 case of constipation, and 1 case of sub-ileus.


asthenia, fatigue, malaise


In a pooled analysis of adults receiving moderately or highly emetogenic chemotherapy (MEC or HEC) in clinical trials, fatigue was reported in 13% of subjects treated with oral aprepitant and 15% of subjects receiving IV fosaprepitant. Fatigue was reported in 5% of pediatric patients who received MEC and HEC in pooled analysis.  Fatigue was reported in 6% of subjects receiving IV aprepitant for the prevention of post-operative nausea and vomiting (PONV). Additional general adverse effects reported in adults receiving aprepitant or fosaprepitant included asthenia (4% to 7%) and malaise (more than 0.5%).


abnormal dreams, agitation, anxiety, dysgeusia, euphoria, lethargy, neurotoxicity, peripheral neuropathy


In a pooled analysis of adults receiving moderately (MEC) and highly emetogenic chemotherapy (HEC) in clinical trials, peripheral neuropathy, dysgeusia, and anxiety were reported in more than 0.5% of subjects treated with a regimen containing oral aprepitant and at a greater incidence than with standard therapy. In trials of subjects receiving IV fosaprepitant as part of an antiemetic regimen with MEC or HEC, peripheral neuropathy occurred in 3% of subjects compared with 2% of those receiving standard therapy. These side effects have not been reported in pediatric patients in MEC and HEC trials. The following adverse events were reported in less than 1% of subjects receiving the 3-day oral aprepitant regimen for MEC, but with a greater incidence than subjects receiving standard therapy: abnormal dreams, cognitive disorder, lethargy, disorientation, euphoria, and somnolence. Use of fosaprepitant or aprepitant may augment the risk for adverse reactions with ifosfamide. During clinical trials, the use of aprepitant with ifosfamide chemotherapy resulted in behavioral changes in two of 49 pediatric patients receiving ifosfamide concurrently (agitation, n = 1; abnormal behavior, n = 1). In postmarketing experience with aprepitant and fosaprepitant, ifosfamide-induced neurotoxicity has been reported during coadministration in both adults and pediatric patients.


dehydration, elevated hepatic enzymes, hypokalemia, hyponatremia, proteinuria


Metabolic, nutritional, and changes in laboratory investigations occurring in the aprepitant-containing regimen at a rate higher than with standard therapy in pooled analysis of adults receiving moderately and highly emetogenic chemotherapy (MEC or HEC) included dehydration (3%). Laboratory investigation changes occurring in more than 0.5% of adults receiving aprepitant for MEC or HEC, and at rates higher than standard therapy included: hypokalemia, elevated hepatic enzymes (e.g., alanine aminotransferase increased in 3%, aspartate aminotransferase increased in 0.5%), blood alkaline phosphatase increased, blood sodium decreased, blood urea increased, and proteinuria. Hyponatremia was reported in less than 1% of subjects receiving the 3-day oral aprepitant regimen for MEC, but with a greater incidence than subjects receiving standard therapy.


anemia, leukopenia, neutropenia, thrombocytopenia


Blood and hematologic effects have been reported during clinical trials with aprepitant and fosaprepitant. In adults receiving moderately and highly emetogenic chemotherapy (MEC or HEC) during clinical trials, neutropenia (8%), anemia (3%), and leukopenia (2%) occurred in subjects treated with a fosaprepitant regimen and a decreased white count (non-neutropenic) occurred in 4% of subjects receiving oral aprepitant; these side effects occurred at incidences higher than with standard therapy in the respective pooled analyses. Anemia, febrile neutropenia, neutropenia, and thrombocytopenia were reported in adults receiving oral aprepitant for MEC or HEC at incidences of more than 0.5%. In pediatric trials for MEC and HEC, 13% of those receiving aprepitant and ondansetron experienced neutropenia, compared with 11% of those who received ondansetron alone. Anemia, neutropenia, thrombocytopenia, and febrile neutropenia were the most common adverse reactions associated with IV fosaprepitant in pediatric trials for MEC and HEC, occurring in more than 15% of subjects. Decreased hemoglobin (5%) was reported in pediatric patients receiving aprepitant with ondansetron vs. 4% of those receiving ondansetron alone for MEC or HEC.


candidiasis, chills, conjunctivitis, cough, infection, pharyngitis, sneezing, throat irritation


Infection and related manifestations were reported as potential side effects of aprepitant, fosaprepitant when used in subjects receiving chemotherapy. Urinary tract infection occurred in 2% of subjects treated with a fosaprepitant regimen in trials for moderate to highly emetogenic chemotherapy (MEC or HEC), and at an incidence higher than with standard therapy (1%). Oral candidiasis, cough, and pharyngitis were reported in more than 0.5% of adults, and cough was reported in 5% of pediatric patients receiving a regimen containing oral aprepitant during MEC and HEC trials; these side effects occurred at incidences higher than with the control regimen. The following adverse events were reported in less than 1% of subjects receiving the 3-day oral aprepitant regimen for MEC, but with a greater incidence than subjects receiving standard therapy: staphylococcal infection, conjunctivitis, sneezing, postnasal drip, throat irritation, and chills.


bradycardia, palpitations


Cardiac side effects have been reported with aprepitant. Palpitations occurred in 0.5% but less than 1% of adults treated with an oral aprepitant-containing regimen during trials for moderate or highly emetogenic chemotherapy (MEC or HEC) at an incidence higher than with standard therapy; bradycardia and cardiovascular disorder were also reported in less than 1% of subjects. Hypotension has been reported with fosaprepitant or aprepitant IV infusions and may indicate a hypersensitivity infusion reaction in some subjects. Chest discomfort was reported in less than 1% of subjects receiving the 3-day oral aprepitant regimen for MEC, but with a greater incidence than subjects receiving standard therapy. After administration, fosaprepitant is rapidly converted to aprepitant; adverse reactions for fosaprepitant are expected to be similar to aprepitant.


hot flashes


In a pooled analysis of adults receiving moderate and highly emetogenic chemotherapy (MEC and HEC) in clinical trials, hot flashes (also reported as flushing) were reported in more than 0.5% of subjects treated with an oral aprepitant-containing regimen and at a greater incidence than those who received standard therapy. These effects were not observed in pediatric patients in trials for MEC or HEC. After administration, fosaprepitant is rapidly converted to aprepitant; adverse reactions for fosaprepitant are expected to be similar to those experienced in subjects receiving aprepitant. Flushing that occurs during fosaprepitant or aprepitant infusion may indicate a hypersensitivity infusion reaction.


muscle cramps, musculoskeletal pain, myalgia


In a pooled analysis of adults receiving moderately (MEC) and highly emetogenic chemotherapy (HEC) in clinical trials, musculoskeletal pain was reported in more than 0.5% of subjects treated with a regimen containing aprepitant and at a greater incidence than those who received standard therapy. In a pooled analysis of trials of subjects receiving IV fosaprepitant regimens for MEC or HEC, pain in extremities occurred in 2% of subjects compared with 1% of those treated with standard treatment. Musculoskeletal side effects were not reported in pediatric trials for MEC or HEC. The following adverse events were reported in less than 1% of subjects receiving the 3-day oral aprepitant regimen for MEC, but with a greater incidence than subjects receiving standard therapy: muscle cramps, myalgia, gait disturbance, and muscular weakness.


peripheral edema


In a pooled analysis of adults receiving moderately (MEC) and highly emetogenic chemotherapy (HEC) in clinical trials, peripheral edema was reported in more than 0.5% of adults treated with an aprepitant regimen and at a greater incidence than those who received standard therapy. Similar reactions would be expected with fosaprepitant.


alopecia, hyperhidrosis


Infrequent dermatologic reactions reported from pooled studies in adults receiving oral aprepitant for moderate or highly emetogenic chemotherapy (MEC or HEC) include hyperhidrosis, rash, and alopecia (each occurred in more than 0.5% of subjects). After administration, fosaprepitant is rapidly converted to aprepitant; adverse reactions for fosaprepitant are expected to be similar to those experienced in subjects receiving aprepitant.


dysuria, hematuria, polyuria


The following adverse events were reported in less than 1% of subjects receiving the 3-day oral aprepitant regimen for MEC, but with a greater incidence than subjects receiving standard therapy: polyuria, dysuria, microscopic hematuria, and pollakiuria.


Description

Aprepitant, fosaprepitant, an antiemetic, is a highly selective substance P neurokinin 1 (NK1) receptor antagonist. Aprepitant is available in oral and intravenous formulations; fosaprepitant, the prodrug of aprepitant, is only available as an intravenous formulation. Aprepitant (oral and IV) and injectable fosaprepitant are approved for use in combination with standard antiemetic regimens (5HT3 antagonist plus a corticosteroid) to prevent acute and delayed chemotherapy-induced nausea and vomiting (CINV) associated with highly emetogenic regimens and delayed CINV associated with moderately emetogenic regimens. Intravenous aprepitant (Aponvie injectable emulsion) is approved for the prevention of post-operative nausea and vomiting (PONV). While aprepitant and fosaprepitant were previously FDA-approved to treat PONV, the manufacturers removed this indication from the labels in September 2019. The American Society of Clinical Oncology (ASCO) guidelines recommend that adults who are treated with moderate to high-emetic-risk chemotherapy agents should be offered a 3-drug combination of a 5-HT3 receptor antagonist, a neurokinin 1 (NK1) receptor antagonist, and dexamethasone; olanzapine is also added to the 3-drug combination during use of high-emetic-risk agents. Children receiving moderate to high-emetic-risk agents should be offered a 2-drug combination of a 5-HT3 receptor antagonist and dexamethasone; aprepitant is also added to the 2-drug combination during use of high-emetic-risk agents. Aprepitant and fosaprepitant were first FDA-approved in March 2003 and January 2008, respectively.

Mechanism Of Action

Fosaprepitant is a prodrug of aprepitant and accordingly, its antiemetic effects are attributable to aprepitant. Aprepitant is a selective high-affinity antagonist of human substance P/neurokinin 1 (NK1) receptors. Aprepitant has little or no affinity for serotonin (5HT3), dopamine, and corticosteroid receptors. Aprepitant has been shown in animal models to inhibit emesis induced by cytotoxic chemotherapeutic agents, such as cisplatin, via central actions. Animal and human Positron Emission Tomography (PET) studies with aprepitant have shown that it crosses the blood brain barrier and occupies brain NK1 receptors. Animal and human studies show that aprepitant augments the antiemetic activity of the 5HT3-receptor antagonist ondansetron and the corticosteroid dexamethasone and inhibits both the acute and delayed phases of cisplatin-induced emesis.

Pharmacokinetics

Aprepitant is administered orally and intravenously; fosaprepitant is administered intravenously. Aprepitant is more than 95% bound to plasma proteins. The mean apparent Vd at steady state was approximately 70 L. Aprepitant is primarily metabolized by CYP3A4 via oxidation at the morpholine ring and its side chains, with minor metabolism via CYP1A2 and CYP2C19. Seven metabolites have been identified, but their clinical activity is limited. Aprepitant crosses the blood brain barrier. Aprepitant is eliminated primarily by metabolism and is not renally excreted. The terminal half-life is 9 to 13 hours.
 
Affected cytochrome P450 isoenzymes and drug transporters: CYP1A2, CYP2C9, CYP2C19, CYP3A4
Aprepitant is a substrate of CYP3A4 and with minor metabolism via CYP1A2 and CYP2C19. No metabolism by CYP2D6, CYP2C9, or CYP2E1 has been detected. Aprepitant is a moderate CYP3A4 inhibitor after multi-day dosing (125 mg/80 mg/80 mg), and exhibits weak CYP3A4 inhibition after a single 40 mg dose. Fosaprepitant 150 mg IV as a single dose is also a weak CYP3A4 inhibitor, which may continue for 2 days after administration; there was no evidence of CYP3A4 inhibition or induction on day 4 after fosaprepitant administration. Aprepitant is also an inducer of CYP3A4 and CYP2C9.

Route-Specific Pharmacokinetics
Oral Route

The pharmacokinetics of aprepitant were non-linear, with an increase in AUC 26% greater than dose-proportional between 80 and 125 mg. After a multi-day regimen of aprepitant (125 mg/80 mg/80 mg), the AUC was 19.6 mcg x hour/mL on day 1 and 21.2 mcg x hour/mL on day 3. The Cmax of 1.6 mcg/mL and 1.4 mcg/mL were reached in approximately 4 hours (Tmax) on day 1 and day 3, respectively. The mean bioavailability was 60% to 65% at a dose range of 80 to 125 mg; food did not affect bioavailability. After a single 40-mg dose of aprepitant, the mean AUC was 7.8 mcg x hour/mL, with a Cmax of 0.7 mcg/mL; Tmax was 3 hours. Absolute bioavailability of the 40-mg dose has not been determined.

Intravenous Route

Fosaprepitant is rapidly converted to aprepitant after administration (within 30 minutes); in vitro, conversion occurs in multiple extrahepatic tissues, including the kidney, lung, and ileum, in addition to the liver. After infusion of a single dose of fosaprepitant 150 mg IV over 20 minutes, the mean aprepitant AUC was 37.4 (+/- 14.8) mcg x hour/mL, and the mean aprepitant Cmax was 4.2 (+/- 1.2) mcg/mL. Plasma concentrations of fosaprepitant were undetectable within 30 minutes of completion of the infusion. After infusion of a single dose of aprepitant 100 mg IV over 30 minutes, the mean aprepitant AUC was 27.8 (+/- 6.5) mcg x hour/mL, and the mean aprepitant Cmax was 4.3 (+/- 1.2) mcg/mL. After infusion of a single dose of aprepitant 130 mg IV over 30 minutes, the mean aprepitant AUC was 43.9 (+/- 12.7) mcg x hour/mL, and the mean aprepitant Cmax was 6.1 (+/- 1.5) mcg/mL. Following administration of a single dose of aprepitant 32 mg IV over 30 minutes, the mean aprepitant AUC was 7.8 (27.4%) mcg x hour/mL, and the mean plasma concentration at 5 minutes post-dose was 2.1 (19%) mcg/mL.

Hepatic Impairment

The pharmacokinetics of aprepitant in subjects with mild and moderate hepatic impairment (Child-Pugh score 5 to 9) were similar to those of healthy subjects with normal hepatic function. There are no clinical or pharmacokinetic data in subjects with severe hepatic impairment (Child-Pugh score more than 9). The AUC of aprepitant was 11% lower on day 1 and 36% lower on day 3 in subjects with mild hepatic impairment (Child-Pugh score 5 to 6), compared to healthy subjects, after administration of a 3-day aprepitant regimen (125 mg/80 mg/80 mg); in subjects with moderate hepatic impairment (Child Pugh score 7 to 9), the AUC was 10% higher on day 1 and 18% higher on day 3, compared to healthy subjects. These differences are not considered clinically meaningful. There are no clinical or pharmacokinetic data in subjects with severe hepatic impairment (Child-Pugh score greater than 9).
 
Aponvie (aprepitant) injection for intravenous injection
No clinically meaningful changes in the pharmacokinetics of aprepitant are expected in subjects with mild or moderate hepatic impairment following administration of a single dose of aprepitant 32 mg as an intravenous injection.

Renal Impairment

The pharmacokinetics of aprepitant in subjects with severe renal impairment and end stage renal disease (ESRD) requiring hemodialysis were similar to those of healthy subjects with normal renal function. After a single dose of aprepitant 240 mg was administered to subjects with severe renal impairment (CrCl less than 30 mL/minute/1.73 m2), the AUC and Cmax of total aprepitant (unbound and protein bound) were decreased by 21% and 32%, respectively, compared to healthy subjects (CrCl more than 80 mL/minute). The protein binding of aprepitant is modestly decreased in subjects with renal disease; therefore, the AUC of unbound aprepitant (pharmacologically active) is not significantly affected in subjects with renal impairment compared to healthy subjects. In subjects with ESRD undergoing hemodialysis, the AUC and Cmax decreased by 42% and 32%, respectively; the timing of hemodialysis (4 hours or 48 hours after dosing) did not significantly affect aprepitant pharmacokinetics. Less than 0.2% of the dose was recovered in dialysate.
 
Aponvie (aprepitant) injection for intravenous injection
No clinically meaningful changes in the pharmacokinetics of aprepitant are expected in subjects with renal impairment following administration of a single dose of aprepitant 32 mg as an intravenous injection.

Pediatrics

Infants and Children 6 to 23 months
The mean 24-hour AUC and Cmax on Day 1 of a 3-day oral aprepitant regimen (3 mg/kg on Day 1, 2 mg/kg on Days 2 and 3) were 20.9 mcg x hour/mL and 1.8 mcg/mL, respectively. The Cmax occurred at approximately 6 hours. Mean concentrations at the end of Day 2 and Day 3 were 0.4 mcg/mL and 0.5 mcg/mL, respectively. Observed AUC and Cmax of aprepitant were 32.7 mcg x hour/mL and 3.3 mcg/mL, respectively, after a single 5 mg/kg IV dose; concentrations at the end of Day 1 were 0.4 mcg/mL. With the 3-day IV/oral/oral regimen, simulated AUC and Cmax were 16.6 mcg x hour/mL and 1.9 mcg/mL, respectively; concentrations at the end of Day 1, Day 2, and Day 3 were 0.2, 0.1, and 0.1 mcg/mL, respectively. With the 3-day IV/IV/IV regimen, simulated AUC and Cmax were 16.7 mcg x hour/mL and 1.9 mcg/mL, respectively; concentrations at the end of Day 1, Day 2, and Day 3 were 0.2, 0.2, and 0.2 mcg/mL, respectively.
 
Children 2 to 5 years
The mean 24-hour AUC and Cmax on Day 1 of a 3-day oral aprepitant regimen (3 mg/kg on Day 1, 2 mg/kg on Days 2 and 3) were 20.9 mcg x hour/mL and 1.8 mcg/mL, respectively. The Cmax occurred at approximately 6 hours. Mean concentrations at the end of Day 2 and Day 3 were 0.4 mcg/mL and 0.5 mcg/mL, respectively. Simulated AUC and Cmax of aprepitant were 28.2 mcg x hour/mL and 3.1 mcg/mL, respectively, after a single 4 mg/kg IV dose; concentrations at the end of Day 1 were 0.4 mcg/mL. With the 3-day IV/oral/oral regimen, simulated AUC and Cmax were 20.2 mcg x hour/mL and 2.3 mcg/mL, respectively; concentrations at the end of Day 1, Day 2, and Day 3 were 0.3, 0.2, and 0.2 mcg/mL, respectively. With the 3-day IV/IV/IV regimen, simulated AUC and Cmax were 20.3 mcg x hour/mL and 2.3 mcg/mL, respectively; concentrations at the end of Day 1, Day 2, and Day 3 were 0.3, 0.2, and 0.2 mcg/mL, respectively.
 
Children 6 to 11 years
The mean 24-hour AUC and Cmax on Day 1 of a 3-day oral aprepitant regimen (3 mg/kg on Day 1, 2 mg/kg on Days 2 and 3) were 20.9 mcg x hour/mL and 1.8 mcg/mL, respectively. The Cmax occurred at approximately 6 hours. Mean concentrations at the end of Day 2 and Day 3 were 0.4 mcg/mL and 0.5 mcg/mL, respectively. Simulated AUC and Cmax of aprepitant were 35.2 mcg x hour/mL and 3.6 mcg/mL, respectively, after a single 4 mg/kg IV dose; concentrations at the end of Day 1 were 0.7 mcg/mL. With the 3-day IV/oral/oral regimen, simulated AUC and Cmax were 25.7 mcg x hour/mL and 2.7 mcg/mL, respectively; concentrations at the end of Day 1, Day 2, and Day 3 were 0.5, 0.3, and 0.3 mcg/mL, respectively. With the 3-day IV/IV/IV regimen, simulated AUC and Cmax were 25.6 mcg x hour/mL and 2.7 mcg/mL, respectively; concentrations at the end of Day 1, Day 2, and Day 3 were 0.5, 0.4, and 0.4 mcg/mL, respectively.
 
Children and Adolescents 12 to 17 years
The mean 24-hour AUC and Cmax on Day 1 of a 3-day aprepitant regimen (125 mg on Day 1, 80 mg on Days 2 and 3) were 17 mcg x hour/mL and 1.3 mcg/mL, respectively. The Cmax occurred at approximately 4 hours. Body weight appears to be a significant covariate for clearance of aprepitant in pediatric patients older than 6 years. The median clearance was 4.4 L/hour in pediatric patients 12 to 17 years and 4.8 L/hour in subjects less than 12 years weighing 30 kg or more. Observed AUC and Cmax of aprepitant were 29.4 mcg x hour/mL and 3.4 mcg/mL, respectively, after a single 150 mg IV dose; concentrations at the end of Day 1 were 0.7 mcg/mL. With the 3-day IV/oral/oral regimen, observed AUC and Cmax were 18 mcg x hour/mL and 3 mcg/mL, respectively; concentrations at the end of Day 1 and Day 2 were 0.4 and 0.2 mcg/mL, respectively (Day 3 was not estimated). With the 3-day IV/IV/IV regimen, observed AUC and Cmax were 21.1 mcg x hour/mL and 2.5 mcg/mL, respectively; concentrations at the end of Days 1, 2 and 3 were 0.4 mcg/mL.

Geriatric

The AUC of aprepitant was 21% higher on day 1 and 36% higher on day 5 in geriatric patients (65 years and older) compared to younger adults after administration of 3-day dosing (125 mg/80 mg/80 mg); the Cmax was 10% higher on day 1 and 24% higher on day 5 in geriatric patients. These differences are not considered clinically meaningful.

Gender Differences

After administration of a single oral dose of aprepitant (40 to 375 mg), the AUC and Cmax of aprepitant were 9% and 17% higher in females compared with males; the half-life was also 25% lower in females. These differences are not considered clinically meaningful.

Ethnic Differences

After administration of a single oral dose of aprepitant (40 to 375 mg), the AUC and Cmax of aprepitant were 27% and 19% higher in Hispanic subjects compared with White subjects; the AUC and Cmax were 74% and 47% higher in Asian subjects compared with White subjects. There was no difference in AUC or Cmax between White and Black subjects. These differences are not considered clinically meaningful.

Obesity

The AUC and Cmax of aprepitant decrease by 9% and 10% for every 5 kg/m2 increase in body mass index (BMI); BMI of subjects in the analysis ranged from 18 kg/m2 to 36 kg/m2. This change is not considered clinically meaningful.

Administration

For storage information, see the specific product information within the How Supplied section.

Oral Administration

Chemotherapy induced nausea and vomiting (CINV)
Administer aprepitant 1 hour prior to chemotherapy on days 1, 2, and 3, or if no chemotherapy is scheduled on days 2 and 3, give aprepitant in the morning.


Oral Solid Formulations

Aprepitant Capsules
May be administered without regard to meals.
Swallow capsules whole.


Oral Liquid Formulations

Aprepitant oral suspension kit
All doses should be prepared by a health care professional.
Each pouch of aprepitant for oral suspension contains 125 mg of aprepitant which is to be suspended in 4.6 mL of water giving a final concentration of 25 mg/mL.
Do not open the pouch of aprepitant until ready to prepare the medicine.
Fill the mixing cup (provided in the kit) with room temperature drinking water.
Using the oral dosing dispenser (syringe provided in the kit), withdraw 4.6 mL of water from the mixing cup. Ensure no air is in the dispenser. Discard remaining water in the mixing cup.
Add the 4.6 mL of water from the dispenser back into the empty mixing cup.
Hold the aprepitant for oral suspension pouch upright and shake the contents to the bottom before opening the pouch.
Pour the entire contents of the pouch into the 4.6 mL of water in the mixing cup and snap the lid shut.
Mix the aprepitant suspension gently by swirling 20 times; then gently invert the mixing cup 5 times. Do NOT shake the mixing cup or foaming may occur. The mixture will be a cloudy pink to light pink.
Check aprepitant suspension for clumps or foaming. If clumps are present, mix the suspension again by gently swirling and inverting the mixing cup. If foam is present, wait for the foam to disappear before continuing.
Chose the dispenser based on the prescribed dose. Use the 1 mL dispenser if dose is 1 mL or less; use 5 mL dispenser if dose is more than 1 mL.
Fill the dispenser with the prescribed dose from the cup. If the dose is less than 1 mL round to the nearest 0.1 mL. If the dose is more than 1 mL round to the nearest 0.2 mL. It is common to have medicine remaining in the cup. Discard the mixing cup along with any remaining suspension.
Make sure no air is present and the dispenser contains the proper dose. Place the cap on the dispenser until it clicks.
Storage: If not administered immediately, store in the refrigerator between 36 to 46 degrees F (2 to 8 degrees C) for up to 72 hours prior to use. When dispensing dose(s) to the caregiver, instruct them to refrigerate the oral dosing dispenser(s) until they are ready to administer the dose. When ready to use, the mixture can be kept at room temperature between 68 to 77 degrees F (20 to 25 degrees C) for up to 3 hours.


Injectable Administration


Visually inspect parenteral products for particulate matter and discoloration prior to administration whenever solution and container permit.


Intravenous Administration

Emend (fosaprepitant) and Navitrux (fosaprepitant) 150-mg powder for injection
Administer by IV infusion only.
Do not mix or reconstitute with any solution containing divalent cations (e.g., calcium or magnesium), including Lactated Ringer's Injection or Hartmann's Solution.
Reconstitution and IV Infusion Preparation
Use aseptic technique to prepare the infusion.
Inject 5 mL 0.9% Sodium Chloride for Injection into the vial, directing the saline towards the wall of the vial to prevent foaming. Avoid jetting the saline into the vial. Swirl the vial gently; avoid shaking. When preparing Navitrux, visually inspect the reconstituted solution in the vial for particulate matter and discoloration prior to further dilution; the solution should be clear and colorless. Discard if particulate matter is present or the solution is discolored.
Prepare an infusion bag filled with 145 mL of 0.9% Sodium Chloride for Injection.
Withdraw the entire volume from the vial (5 mL) and transfer to the infusion bag (total infusion volume = 150 mL; final concentration = 1 mg/mL).
Gently invert the bag 2 to 3 times.
Determine the volume to be administered from the prepared infusion bag, based on the individual patient's dose. In adults, administer the entire volume of the prepared infusion bag (150 mL). In pediatric patients, the entire volume of the infusion bag may not be required. For volumes less than 150 mL, the calculated volume can be transferred to an appropriate size bag or syringe prior to administration.
Storage:
Emend: The prepared IV infusion is stable for 24 hours at ambient room temperature, at or below 25 degrees C (77 degrees F). Discard unused portion.
Navitrux: Store the reconstituted fosaprepitant and final diluted solution at ambient room temperature [at or below 25 degrees C (77 degrees F)] for no longer than 24 hours from the time of reconstitution. Discard any unused reconstituted or diluted solution after 24 hours.
IV Infusion Administration
Avoid infusion into small veins or through a butterfly catheter. Closely monitor individuals during and after the infusion. Do not reinitiate in individuals who experience infusion site reactions with first-time use.
Adults: Administer over 20 to 30 minutes, completing the infusion approximately 30 minutes prior to chemotherapy.
Children and Adolescents 12 to 17 years: Administer over 30 minutes, completing the infusion approximately 30 minutes prior to chemotherapy.
Infants and Children 6 months to 11 years: Administer over 60 minutes, completing the infusion approximately 30 minutes prior to chemotherapy.
 
Focinvez (fosaprepitant) 150-mg solution for injection
Administer by IV infusion only.
Do not use if solution is cloudy, contains precipitates, or the red flip top cap is not intact.
Allow fosaprepitant to come to room temperature before use if the injection is not stored at room temperature.
IV Infusion Preparation
Focinvez is ready-to-use for IV infusion. The concentration is 3 mg/mL.
Determine the volume to be administered from the injection vial directly based on the recommended dose.
Use a vented infusion set when infusing from the bottle.
Gently invert the bottle and hang using the attached hanger and start infusion. The vial hanger can be separated from the vial label and hanged from an IV pole.
Focinvez is compatible with 0.9% Sodium Chloride for Injection. Do not mix or reconstitute with any solution containing divalent cations (e.g., calcium or magnesium), including Lactated Ringer's Injection or Hartmann's Solution.
IV Infusion Administration
Avoid infusion into small veins or through a butterfly catheter. Closely monitor individuals during and after the infusion. Do not reinitiate in individuals who experience infusion site reactions with first-time use.
Adults: Administer the entire volume of the vial (50 mL) over 20 to 30 minutes, completing the infusion approximately 30 minutes prior to chemotherapy.
Children and Adolescents 12 to 17 years: The volume to administer (mL) = the recommended dose (mg)/ 3 (mg/mL). The entire volume in the vial may not be required; discard the unused portion. Administer over 30 minutes, completing the infusion approximately 30 minutes prior to chemotherapy.
Infants and Children 6 months to 11 years: The volume to administer (mL) = the recommended dose (mg/kg) X weight (kg)/ 3 (mg/mL). The entire volume in the vial may not be required.; discard the unused portion. Administer over 60 minutes, completing the infusion approximately 30 minutes prior to chemotherapy.
Following administration, flush the IV set with a sufficient volume of 0.9% Sodium Chloride Injection to ensure the full length of the tubing and catheter lumen is completely cleared of residual medication to prevent precipitation. Proceed with the administration of other medications through the same line only after the line has been completely flushed.
 
Cinvanti (aprepitant) and generic aprepitant 130-mg emulsion for IV infusion
IV Infusion Preparation
Use aseptic technique to prepare the infusion.
Prepare an infusion bag filled with 100 mL of 0.9% Sodium Chloride for Injection or 5% Dextrose for Injection.
Withdraw the entire volume from the vial (18 mL) and transfer to the infusion bag.
Gently invert the bag 4 to 5 times. Avoid shaking.
Storage: The reconstituted final drug solution is stable at ambient room temperature for 6 hours in 0.9% Sodium Chloride Injection or 12 hours in 5% Dextrose Injection or up to 72 hours if stored under refrigeration in 0.9% Sodium Chloride Injection or 5% Dextrose Injection.
IV Infusion Administration
Administer over 30 minutes.
 
Cinvanti (aprepitant) and generic aprepitant 130-mg emulsion for IV injection
IV Administration and Preparation
Use aseptic technique to prepare the injection.
Draw 18 mL from the vial. Do not dilute.
The infusion line should be flushed with 0.9% Sodium Chloride Injection before and after administration.
Administer IV over 2 minutes.
 
Cinvanti (aprepitant) and generic aprepitant 100-mg emulsion for IV infusion
IV Infusion Preparation
Use aseptic technique to prepare the IV infusion.
Prepare an infusion bag filled with 100 mL of 0.9% Sodium Chloride for Injection or 5% Dextrose for Injection.
Withdraw 14 mL from the vial and transfer to the infusion bag.
Gently invert the bag 4 to 5 times. Avoid shaking.
Storage: The reconstituted final drug solution is stable at ambient room temperature for 6 hours in 0.9% Sodium Chloride Injection or 12 hours in 5% Dextrose Injection or up to 72 hours if stored under refrigeration in 0.9% Sodium Chloride Injection or 5% Dextrose Injection.
IV infusion Administration
Administer over 30 minutes.
 
Cinvanti (aprepitant) and generic aprepitant 100-mg emulsion for IV injection
IV Administration and Preparation
Use aseptic technique to prepare the injection.
Draw 14 mL from the vial. Do not dilute.
The infusion line should be flushed with 0.9% Sodium Chloride Injection before and after administration.
Administer IV over 2 minutes.
 
Aponvie (aprepitant) emulsion for IV injection
IV Administration and Preparation
Use aseptic technique to prepare the injection.
Draw 4.4 mL from the vial. Do not dilute.
The infusion line should be flushed with 0.9% Sodium Chloride Injection before and after administration.
Aponvie is compatible with 0.9% Sodium Chloride Injection, USP or 5% Dextrose Injection, USP, and solutions containing divalent cations (e.g., calcium, magnesium), including Lactated Ringer's Solution.
Administer IV over 30 seconds.


Maximum Dosage Limits
Adults

125 mg/day PO (Day 1) and 80 mg/day PO (Days 2 and 3); 150 mg/day fosaprepitant IV (Day 1) (Emend, Focinvez, and Navitrux products).
130 mg/day aprepitant IV emulsion (Cinvanti and generic aprepitant injectable emulsion) (Day 1) for CINV prophylaxis.
32 mg/day aprepitant IV emulsion (Aponvie) for PONV prophylaxis.

Geriatric

125 mg/day PO (Day 1) and 80 mg/day PO (Days 2 and 3); 150 mg/day fosaprepitant IV (Day 1) (Emend, Focinvez, and Navitrux products).
130 mg/day aprepitant IV emulsion (Cinvanti and generic aprepitant injectable emulsion) (Day 1) for CINV prophylaxis.
32 mg/day aprepitant IV emulsion (Aponvie) for PONV prophylaxis.

Adolescents

125 mg/day PO (Day 1) and 80 mg/day PO (Days 2 and 3); 150 mg/day fosaprepitant IV (Day 1) for a single-dose CINV regimen; 115 mg/day fosaprepitant IV (Day 1) for a multiple-dose CINV regimen; safety and efficacy of the IV aprepitant emulsion have not been established.

Children

12 years: 125 mg/day PO (Day 1) and 80 mg/day PO (Days 2 and 3); 150 mg/day fosaprepitant IV (Day 1) for a single-dose CINV regimen; 115 mg/day fosaprepitant IV (Day 1) for a multiple-dose CINV regimen; safety and efficacy of the IV aprepitant emulsion have not been established.
2 to 11 years: 3 mg/kg/day PO (Max: 125 mg/day - Day 1) and 2 mg/kg/day PO (Max: 80 mg/day - Days 2 and 3); 4 mg/kg/day fosaprepitant IV (Max: 150 mg/day - Day 1) for a single-dose CINV regimen; 3 mg/kg/day fosaprepitant IV (Max: 115 mg/day - Day 1) for a multiple-dose CINV regimen; safety and efficacy of the IV aprepitant emulsion have not been established.
1 year: 3 mg/kg/day PO (Max: 125 mg/day - Day 1) and 2 mg/kg/day PO (Max: 80 mg/day - Days 2 and 3); 5 mg/kg/day fosaprepitant IV (Max: 150 mg/day - Day 1) for a single-dose CINV regimen; 3 mg/kg/day fosaprepitant IV (Max: 115 mg/day - Day 1) for a multiple-dose CINV regimen; safety and efficacy of the IV aprepitant emulsion have not been established.

Infants

6 to 11 months: 3 mg/kg/day PO (Day 1) and 2 mg/kg/day PO (Days 2 and 3); 5 mg/kg/day fosaprepitant IV (Day 1) for a single-dose CINV regimen; 3 mg/kg/day fosaprepitant IV (Day 1) for a multiple-dose CINV regimen; safety and efficacy of the IV aprepitant emulsion have not been established.
1 to 5 months: Safety and efficacy have not been established for any dosage form.

Dosage Forms

Aprepitant 125mg Oral capsule, Aprepitant 80mg Oral capsule

Emend 80mg Capsule BiPack

Dosage Adjustment Guidelines
Hepatic Impairment

Mild to moderate hepatic impairment (Child-Pugh score, 5 to 9): No adjustment needed.
Severe impairment (Child-Pugh score more than 9): There are no clinical data in people with severe hepatic impairment; therefore, additional monitoring may be warranted with use.

Renal Impairment

No dosage adjustment is necessary for patients with any degree of renal impairment.
